Apologies from Japanese officials have faced scrutiny for their lack of sincerity.[^cite_note-tandfonline.com-208] For instance, in the Kono Statement, while acknowledging the Japanese military's role in the comfort women system, officials denied coercion and forced transportation of women, and declined to provide compensation to the victims.[^cite_note-tandfonline.com-208] Kono's apology was meticulous in distinguishing the actions of the Japanese army from those of the Japanese government, ensuring that the Japanese government bore no legal liability or responsibility for its treatment of the comfort women.[^cite_note-The_Cornell_Diplomat-6] Also, many victims viewed the apology as insufficient because it was delivered by the Cabinet Secretary of Japan and never officially adopted by the Japanese Parliament.[^cite_note-209] Subsequent apologies were also criticized as insincere because they were delivered by the current prime minister of Japan rather than the [National Diet](163847.md), which would have signified an apology backed by the Japanese government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuki2013248-210] Subsequent apologies written and signed by the standing prime minister of Japan were distributed by the Asian Women's Fund, which is a [non-governmental organization](46539.md), rendering these apologies unofficial.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuki2013248-210]

Japan has largely disregarded recommendations from the [United Nations Human Rights Council](635790.md), as well as the rulings from the [Women's International War Crimes Tribunal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_International_War_Crimes_Tribunal_on_Japan's_Military_Sexual_Slavery) concerning Japan's military sexual slavery.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuki2013249-211] Japan has resisted pressure from other countries, including the United States and the European Union, who have passed resolutions urging the Japanese government to respond.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuki2013249-211] Instead, Japan has indicated that it does not consider these statements legally binding and therefore does not feel obligated to act upon them.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuki2013249-211]

### Controversies

A 2001 comic book, *[Neo Gomanism Manifesto Special – On Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo_Gomanism_Manifesto_Special_%E2%80%93_On_Taiwan)* by Japanese author [Yoshinori Kobayashi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoshinori_Kobayashi), depicts kimono-clad women lining up to sign up for duty before a Japanese soldier. Kobayashi's book contains an interview with Taiwanese industrialist [Shi Wen-long](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shi_Wen-long), who stated that no women were forced to serve and that the women worked in more hygienic conditions compared to regular prostitutes because the use of [condoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Condom) was mandatory.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELandler2001-212]

Moreover, 'patriotic' emerging faiths in Japan like [Happy Science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Happy_Science) and certain Christian factions advocating for the merging of religion and state have initiated a concerted effort domestically and internationally to deny the existence of the comfort women system. They've gathered extensive support from Japanese citizens who refute the existence of the comfort women issue, alleging it as a concoction by left-leaning factions. Several leaders of these groups are women.[^cite_note-213]

In early 2001, in a controversy involving national public broadcaster [NHK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NHK), what was supposed to be coverage of the [Women's International War Crimes Tribunal on Japan's Military Sexual Slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_International_War_Crimes_Tribunal_on_Japan's_Military_Sexual_Slavery) was heavily edited to reflect revisionist views.[^cite_note-214] In 2014, the new president of NHK compared the wartime Japanese comfort women program to Asian brothels frequented by American troops, which western historians countered by pointing out the difference between the Japanese comfort stations, which forced women to have sex with Japanese troops, and Asian brothels, where women chose to be prostitutes for American troops.[^cite_note-215]

In publications around 2007, Japanese historian and Nihon University professor [Ikuhiko Hata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ikuhiko_Hata) estimates the number of comfort women to have been more likely between 10,000 and 20,000.[^cite_note-AWF_CW10-11-9] Hata claims that "none of the comfort women were forcibly recruited".[^cite_note-216] Historian [Chunghee Sarah Soh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chunghee_Sarah_Soh) noted that Hata's initial estimate was at approximately 90,000, but he reduced that figure to 20,000 for political reasons.[^cite_note-217] He has been criticized by other Japanese scholars for minimizing the hardship of comfort women.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrea200641-218]

In 2012, the former mayor of Osaka and co-leader of the [Japan Restoration Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan_Restoration_Party),[^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220] [Tōru Hashimoto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C5%8Dru_Hashimoto#Views_on_prostitution_and_%22comfort_women%22) initially maintained that "there is no evidence that people called comfort women were taken away by violence or threat by the \[Japanese] military".[^cite_note-221] He later modified his position, asserting that they became comfort women "against their will by any circumstances around them",[^cite_note-HashimotoMay2013-222] still justifying their role during World War II as "necessary", so that soldiers could "have a rest".[^cite_note-HashimotoMay2013-222]

In 2013, Yuzuru Nishida, who was member of House of Representatives in Japan, said that the women voluntarily engaged in prostitution and that the estimated number of comfort women was significantly exaggerated.[^cite_note-223]

In 2014, Foreign Minister [Hirofumi Nakasone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hirofumi_Nakasone) chaired a commission established to consider "concrete measures to restore Japan's honor with regard to the comfort women issue", despite his own father [Yasuhiro Nakasone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasuhiro_Nakasone), having organized a "comfort station" in 1942 when he was a lieutenant paymaster in Japan's Imperial Navy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKotler2014-27]

In 2014, the [Japanese Foreign Ministry](868429.md) attempted to pressure [McGraw Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McGraw_Hill) into erasing several paragraphs on comfort women from one of their textbooks. The attempt was unsuccessful, and American academics criticized Japanese attempts to revise the history of comfort women.[^cite_note-224]

In 2018, the *Japan Times* changed its description of the terms "comfort woman" and "forced labourer" causing a controversy among staff and readers.[^cite_note-225]

On August 18, 2018, United Nations rights experts and UN [Committee on the Elimination of Racial Discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_on_the_Elimination_of_Racial_Discrimination) expressed that Japan should do more for sufferers of wartime sexual slavery. Japan responded by stating it has already made numerous apologies and offered compensation to the victims.\[^cite_note-[www.sbs.com.au-226](http://www.sbs.com.au-226/)]

Japanese politicians have demanded the removal of comfort women monuments located in other countries.[^cite_note-NYT_Semple12-227][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCurry2018-228] They have demanded the removal of comfort women statues in Palisades Park, New Jersey, United States; San Francisco, California, United States; and Berlin, Germany, with each demand rejected by the relevant authorities.[^cite_note-229][^cite_note-230]

In 2019, about 24 members of [Malaya Lolas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaya_Lolas), an organization that supports Filipina survivors of sexual slavery during World War II, filed a complaint at UN's [Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_on_the_Elimination_of_All_Forms_of_Discrimination_Against_Women) regarding the Philippine government's failure to fight for their cause, which resulted in ongoing discrimination against comfort women, that continues to this day.[^cite_note-231] In 2023, CEDAW came up with a decision and recommended the government to provide the complainants with full reparation, including material compensation and an official apology for the continuing discrimination.[^cite_note-232]

Wikisource logo

English [Wikisource](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikisource) has original text related to this article:&#x20;

**[Japanese Prisoner of War Interrogation Report 49](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/en\:Japanese%20Prisoner%20of%20War%20Interrogation%20Report%2049)**

Based on a statement made by Representative [Seijuro Arahune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seijuro_Arahune) of the Japanese Diet in 1975 in which he claimed to cite numbers provided by Korean authorities during the [1965 Korea-Japan Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1965_Korea-Japan_Treaty) negotiations,[^cite_note-233] as many as three-fourths of Korean comfort women may have died during the war. However, according to the Japanese government, the validity of this statement has since been brought into question as the number does not seem to be based on an actual investigation on the matter.[^cite_note-234]

[Mio Sugita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mio_Sugita), who is a member of the Liberal Democratic Party and served as a member of the [House of Representatives](217778.md), dismissed the comfort women issue as a fabrication.[^cite_note-235]

The Japanese government denies evidence that women were coerced into sexual slavery but acknowledges that girls and women suffered.[^cite_note-NYT20250306-7]

#### Asahi Shimbun Third-Party Investigative Committee

In August 2014, the *[Asahi Shimbun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asahi_Shimbun)*, Japan's second largest newspaper in circulation, retracted 16 articles published between 1982 and 1997. The articles were concerned with former imperial army officer [Seiji Yoshida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seiji_Yoshida), who claimed he had forcibly taken Korean women to wartime Japanese military brothels from the [Jeju Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeju_Island) region in South Korea. Following the retraction of the articles, the newspaper also refused to publish an op-ed on the matter by Japanese journalist [Akira Ikegami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akira_Ikegami). The public response and criticism that ensued pushed the newspaper to nominate a third-party investigative committee headed by seven leading scholars, journalists and legal experts. The committee report dealt with the circumstances leading to the publication of Yoshida's false testimony and to the effect these publications had on Japan's image abroad and diplomatic relations with various countries. It found that the Asahi was negligent in publishing Yoshida's testimony, but that the reports on the testimony had "limited" effect on foreign media outlets and reports. On the other hand, the report found that Japanese officials' comments on the issue had a far more detrimental effect on Japan's image and its diplomatic relations.[^cite_note-236]

#### Fraud accusations against support groups

In 2004, 13 former comfort women filed a complaint against the Korean Council for the Women Drafted for Military Sexual Slavery and the House of Sharing with the Seoul Western District Court to prevent these two organizations from profiting and exploiting the victims' past experiences to collect donations. The victims accused Shin Hye-Soo, head of the Korean Council at the time, and Song Hyun-Seob, Head of the House of Sharing, of using the women's past experiences in videos and leaflets without their permission to solicit donations and then keeping the money instead of using it to help the victims. The complaint further stated that a significant number of victims did not receive compensation through the citizen-funded Asian Women's Fund established in 1995 by Japan due to the opposition from the organizations in 1998. In addition, they accused the institutions of recruiting six former comfort women survivors from China and paying them to get them to partake in weekly rallies. The complaint was dismissed by the court in May 2005.[^cite_note-237]

Again, in May 2020, [Lee Yong-soo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Yong-soo_\(activist\)), a comfort woman survivor and longtime activist for the victims, held a press conference and accused the Korean Council and its former head, Yoon Mee-hyang, of exploiting her and other survivors, politically and financially for decades, to obtain government funds and public donations through the protests while spending little money aiding them.[^cite_note-238]

Consequently, a civic group filed a complaint against Yoon Mee-hyang, a lawmaker-elect and former head of the Korean Council for Justice and Remembrance for the Issues of Military Sexual Slavery by Japan. After an investigation, the Seoul Western District Prosecutors' Office indicted Yoon, on eight charges including fraud, embezzlement and breach of trust.

Among the charges, Yoon was indicted for is a count of quasi-fraud against [Gil Won-ok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gil_Won-ok), a 92-year-old survivor. The prosecution said Gil has dementia and that Yoon had exploited her reduced physical and mental capacities and pressed her to donate a total of 79.2 million won (\$67,102) to the Korean Council between November 2017 and January 2020.

Additionally, she was accused of fraud and embezzlement of almost half a million dollars from governmental organizations and private donors, which were used to buy properties and even pay tuition for her daughter's education at the University of California.

In a forensic audit of the comfort women's shelter controlled by Yoon's group, it was found that 2.3% of its \$7.5 million budget raised since 2015 was actually spent on supporting the living needs of surviving comfort women, many of whom live in cramped quarters, with substandard care, with few luxuries.[^cite_note-239][^cite_note-240]

In September 2020, the Democratic Party (DP) suspended Yoon's party membership due to the charges that she was facing.[^cite_note-241][^cite_note-242]

On November 14, 2024, South Korea's Supreme Court upheld the conviction of Yoon Mee-hyang, on charges of embezzlement. Yoon was sentenced to 18 months in prison, with the sentence suspended for three years. The court found her guilty of misappropriating funds intended for supporting victims.[^cite_note-243][^cite_note-244]

#### International Court of Justice

The Comfort Women survivors have asked the Korean government multiple times to bring their case in front of the International Court of Justice, but South Korea has yet to respond.[^cite_note-245]

#### International court cases



Members of the Malaya Lolas held a demonstration with families of Philippine "comfort women" on International Memorial Day for "Comfort Women" (August 14) 2024 in [Manila](184334.md), Philippines.

[^cite_note-246]

Members of the group "Malaya Lolas" in the Philippines have attempted to go to Tokyo to file a suit in the Japanese courts. The lolas were unable to file the lawsuit because, according to the Japanese government, international law required them to be represented by the Philippine government. The lolas filed a case in the Philippine courts, Vinuya et al. v. Executive Secretary et al. The case was directed at the Executive Secretary at the time, Alberto G. Romulo, and the main plaintiff was the leader of Malaya Lolas, Isabelita Vinuya. The lolas filed this case to get the Philippine government to support them in pursuing a petition for compensation in the Japanese courts. The Philippine government won the case with the court stating that the Philippine government "is not under any international obligation to espouse petitioners' claims."[^cite_note-vinuya-247]

### International support

The cause has long been supported beyond the victim nations, and associations like [Amnesty International](18947898.md) are campaigning in countries where governments have yet to support the cause, like in Australia,[^cite_note-248] or New Zealand.[^cite_note-249]

Support in the United States continues to grow, particularly after the passage of [United States House of Representatives House Resolution 121](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives_House_Resolution_121) on July 30, 2007. The resolution expresses that the government of Japan should formally redress the situation by acknowledging, apologizing and accepting historical responsibility for the use of comfort women by its armed forces; have the Prime Minister of Japan give a public apology; and educate their people using internationally accepted historical facts about the crime while refuting any claims that deny the crime.[^cite_note-250] In July 2012, then Secretary of State Hillary Clinton, a strong advocate of the cause, denounced the use of the euphemism 'comfort women' for what should be referred to as 'enforced sex slaves'.[^cite_note-251] The Obama Administration also addressed the need for Japan to do more to address the issue.[^cite_note-252] In addition to calling attention to the issue, the American memorial statues erected in New Jersey in 2010 and California in 2013 show support for what has become an international cause.[^cite_note-latimes1-253]

On November 28, 2007, the [Parliament of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Canada) unanimously passed a motion that recognized Japan's use of women as sex slaves during the Second World War, demanding Japan to make a formal sincere apology to all victims.[^cite_note-254]

In 2007, the Netherlands' [House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Representatives_\(Netherlands\)) passed a resolution that urged Japan to apologize for its wartime sex slavery, and to pay compensations to former comfort women.[^cite_note-255]

On December 13, 2007, the [European Parliament](9581.md) adopted a resolution on "Justice for the 'Comfort Women' (sex slaves in Asia before and during World War II)" calling on the Japanese government to apologise and accept legal responsibility for the coercion of young women into sexual slavery before and during WWII.[^cite_note-256]

In 2014, [Pope Francis](1687680.md) met with seven former comfort women in South Korea.[^cite_note-257][^cite_note-258] Also in 2014, the U.N. Committee on the Elimination of Racial Discrimination called for Japan to, as the committee's deputy head Anastasia Crickley puts it, "conclude investigations into the violations of the rights of 'comfort women' by the military and to bring to justice those responsible and to pursue a comprehensive and lasting resolution to these issues".[^cite_note-japantimes.co.jp-259] U.N. Human Rights Commissioner [Navi Pillay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navi_Pillay) had also spoken out in support of comfort women several times.[^cite_note-japantimes.co.jp-259]

### Health-related issues

In the aftermath of the war, the women recalled bouts of physical and mental abuse that they had experienced while working in military brothels. In the [Rorschach test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rorschach_test), the women showed distorted perceptions, difficulty in managing emotional reactions and internalized anger.[^cite_note-260] A 2011 clinical study found that comfort women were more prone to showing symptoms of [post-traumatic stress disorder](82974.md) (PTSD), even 60 years after the end of the war.[^cite_note-261]

### Survivors

The last surviving victims have become public figures in Korea, where they are referred to as "halmoni", the affectionate term for "grandmother". There is a nursing home, called [House of Sharing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Sharing), for former comfort women in South Korea. China remains more at the testimony collection stage, particularly through the China "Comfort Women" Issue Research Center at [Shanghai Normal University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shanghai_Normal_University),[^cite_note-262] sometimes in collaboration with Korean researchers. For other nations, the research and the interaction with victims is less advanced.

Despite the efforts at assigning responsibility and victims compensation, in the years after World War II, many former Korean comfort women were afraid to reveal their past, because they are afraid of being disowned or ostracized further.[^cite_note-Pilzer2012-263]

## Memorials and organizations

### China

On December 1, 2015, the first memorial hall dedicated to Chinese comfort women was opened in [Nanjing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanjing). It was built on the site of a former comfort station run by the invading Japanese troops during World War II.[^cite_note-264] The memorial hall stands next to the [Memorial Hall of the Victims in Nanjing Massacre by Japanese Invaders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorial_Hall_of_the_Victims_in_Nanjing_Massacre_by_Japanese_Invaders).

In 1999, the Research Center for Chinese Comfort Women was established at [Shanghai Normal University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shanghai_Normal_University).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPan2025156-265] Its museum opened in June 2016.[^cite_note-266]

In 2000, the first international conference on the study of Chinese comfort women was held in Shanghai.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPan2025156-265]

### Taiwan



The [Ama Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ama_Museum) in [Taipei](57648.md) dedicated to Taiwanese comfort women

Since the 1990s, Taiwanese survivors have been bringing to light the comfort woman issue in Taiwanese society, and gaining support from women's rights activists and civil groups. Their testimony and memories have been documented by newspapers, books, and documentary films.

Survivors' claims against the Japan government have been backed by the [Taipei Women's Rescue Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taipei_Women's_Rescue_Foundation) (TWRF) a non-profit organization helping women against violence, and sexual violence. This organization gives legal and psychological support to Taiwanese comfort women, and also helps in the recording of testimony and doing scholarly research. In 2007, this organization was responsible for promoting awareness in society, by creating meetings in universities and high schools where survivors gave their testimonies to students and the general public.[^cite_note-267] TWRF has produced exhibitions that give survivors the opportunity to be heard in Taipei, and also in the Women's Active Museum on War and Peace, based in [Tokyo](30057.md).[^cite_note-268][^cite_note-269]

Thanks to this increasing awareness in society, and with the help of TWRF, Taiwanese comfort women have gained the support their government, which on many occasions has asked the Japanese government for apologies and compensation.[^cite_note-270][^cite_note-271]

In November 2014, "Song of the Reed", a documentary film directed by Wu Hsiu-ching and produced by TWRF, won the International Gold Panda documentary award.[^cite_note-272]

In 2016, a museum dedicated to comfort women, the [Ama Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ama_Museum), opened in [Taipei](57648.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPan2025157-273]

On August 14, 2018, the first 'comfort women' statue in Taiwan was unveiled in the city of [Tainan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tainan).[^cite_note-274] The statue symbolizes women forced to work in wartime brothels for the Japanese military. The bronze statue portrays a girl raising both hands to the sky to express her helpless resistance to suppression and silent protest, according to its creator.[^cite_note-275] In September 2018, Japanese right-wing activist [Mitsuhiko Fujii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitsuhiko_Fujii?action=edit\&redlink=1) kicked the statue and caused outrage in Taiwan, with the Taiwanese government branding his behavior as unacceptable.[^cite_note-276][^cite_note-277][^cite_note-278] A Japanese right-wing group with affiliations to him apologized for his behavior and said he resigned from his group position.[^cite_note-279] In 2024, the land that the statue was on was sold at auction and the statue was put into a warehouse.[^cite_note-280]

### South Korea

#### Wednesday demonstrations

Main article: [Wednesday demonstration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wednesday_demonstration)

Every Wednesday, living comfort women, women's organizations, socio-civic groups, religious groups, and a number of individuals participate in the Wednesday Demonstrations in front of the Japanese Embassy in [Seoul](19159283.md), sponsored by "The Korean Council for the Women Drafted for Military Sexual Slavery by Japan (KCWDMSS)". It was first held on January 8, 1992, when Japan's Prime Minister [Kiichi Miyazawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiichi_Miyazawa) visited South Korea. In December 2011, a [statue of a young woman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statue_of_peace) was erected in front of the Japanese Embassy to honor the comfort women on the 1,000th Wednesday Demonstration. The Japanese government has repeatedly asked the South Korean government to have the statue taken down, but it has not been removed.

On December 28, 2015, the Japanese government claimed that the Korean government agreed to the removal of the statue. As of September 3, 2016, the statue was still in place due to a majority of the South Korean population being opposed to the agreement. On December 30, 2016,[^cite_note-281] another comfort woman statue identical to the one in front of the Japanese Embassy in Seoul was erected in front of the Japanese consulate in [Busan](68115.md), South Korea.[^cite_note-282] As of January 6, 2017, the Japanese government is attempting to negotiate for the removal of the statue. On May 11, 2017, newly elected South Korean President [Moon Jae-in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_Jae-in) announced the agreement would not be enacted in its current stage and that negotiations for a deal between Japan and South Korea over the comfort women dispute had to start over.[^cite_note-may112017-283]

On June 30, 2017, the local government of Busan enacted the legal foundation to protect the Statue of Peace by passing the relative ordinance.[^cite_note-284] By reason of this, it has become difficult to shift the site or demolish the statue.

On August 14, 2018, South Korea held an unveiling ceremony for a monument memorializing Korean women forced to work in wartime brothels for the Japanese military, as the nation observed its first official comfort women memorial day.[^cite_note-285]

On November 21, 2018, South Korea officially cancelled the 2015 agreement and shut down the Japan-funded comfort women foundation which was launched in July 2016 to finance the agreement's settlement to the victims.[^cite_note-286][^cite_note-287] The settlement had received criticism from victims' groups.[^cite_note-may112017-283]

#### House of Sharing

Main article: [House of Sharing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Sharing)

The [House of Sharing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Sharing) is a nursing home for living comfort women. The House of Sharing was founded in June 1992 through funds raised by [Buddhist](3267529.md) organizations and various socio-civic groups and it moved to [Gyeonggi-do](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gyeonggi-do), South Korea in 1998. The House of Sharing includes "The Museum of Sexual Slavery by Japanese Military" to spread the truth about the Japanese military's brutal abuse of comfort women and to educate descendants and the public.[^cite_note-288]

#### Archives by comfort women

Some of the survivors, Kang Duk-kyung, Kim Soon-duk and Lee Yong-Nyeo, preserved their personal history through their drawings as a visual archive.[^cite_note-289] Also, the director of the [Center for Asian American Media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_Asian_American_Media), Dai Sil Kim-Gibson, made a comfort women video archive, a documentary film for K–12 through college level students. Feminist visual and video archives have promoted a place for solidarity between the victims and the public. It has served as a living site for the teaching and learning of women's dignity and human rights by bringing people together despite age, gender, borders, nationality, and ideologies.[^cite_note-290]

### Philippines



Historical Marker, Plaza Lawton, Liwasang Bonifacio, Manila



*[Bahay na Pula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahay_na_Pula)* in [San Ildefonso, Bulacan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Ildefonso,_Bulacan), used as barracks by Japanese soldiers in World War II where young Filipino comfort women were imprisoned and used as sex slaves

Comfort women in the Philippines, called "Lolas" (grandmothers), formed different groups similar to the Korean survivors. One group, named "Lila Pilipina" (League of Filipino Women), started in 1992 and is member of [GABRIELA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GABRIELA), a feminist organization.[^cite_note-gabriela-291] In their brochure they list their demands in two categories, ones to the Japanese government and those for the Philippine government. From the Japanese government Lila Pilipina has five demands:[^cite_note-lila-pilipina-292]

1. "that Japan fulfills its responsibility in the full disclosure of all information in its war archives concerning the operation of the "comfort stations" and the "comfort women" system

2. "adequate compensation for the women victims and their families from the Japanese government,"

3. "for the Japanese government to include as reference in its textbooks and history books the reality of military sexual slavery through "comfort women" during World War II as a crime,"

4. "for the Japanese government to admit the use of force and violence in the conscription and treatment of the "comfort women" as military sex slaves, contrary to Japanese government report,"

5. "a formal apology to the Filipino people and specifically to the women victims and their families for having a direct hand in the conscription of Asian women for military sexual slavery."

From the Philippine government Lila Pilipina also has five demands:[^cite_note-lila-pilipina-292]

1. "to issue an official position declaring the comfort women system as a war crime, condemning the Japanese government in its direct involvement for institutionalized sexual slavery and demanding formal apology and compensation for the victims and their families,"

2. "to conduct an official investigation and documentation of the comfort women issue,"

3. "to include the reality of the "comfort women" and "comfort stations" during World War II in Philippine history. These include the curriculum, textbooks and other instructional materials used both in public and private educational institutions in all levels,"

4. "to build historical markers and shrines around the country for the comfort women and war victims of World War II as a reminder to the present generation of the sad realities behind wars of aggression,"

5. "to provide material support for the victims, survivors, and their families."

Lila Pilipina together with the [Malaya Lolas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaya_Lolas) (Free grandmothers) took legal actions against Japan. These groups also ask the Philippine government to back their claims against the Japanese government.[^cite_note-embassy-293][^cite_note-294] These groups have taken legal actions against Japan.[^cite_note-295] Malaya Lolas had attempted to go to Tokyo to file a suit in the Japanese courts. Still, according to the Japanese government, the lolas themselves could not file a lawsuit due to international law stating that the lolas need to be represented by the Philippine government. In 2004 the Malaya Lolas filed a cause, Vinuya et al. v. Executive Secretary et al., asking for the support of the Philippine government in pursuing a petition for compensation in the Japanese courts. A decision was made on April 28, 2010, supporting the Philippine government.[^cite_note-vinuya-247] As of August 2014, after failing in legal action against their own government to back their claims, they planned to take the case the UN [Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women and Children](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_on_the_Elimination_of_All_Forms_of_Discrimination_Against_Women) (CEDAW).[^cite_note-296]



&#x20;[Flower-for-Lolas Campaign Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaya_Lolas)

These groups have made demonstrations in front of the Japanese embassy in [Manila](184334.md) on many occasions,[^cite_note-embassy-293][^cite_note-297] and have given testimonies to Japanese tourists in Manila.[^cite_note-gabriela-291] The Filipino news channel [ABS-CBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABS-CBN) has done interviews with the surviving lolas to bring awareness to the experience of lolas under the Japanese occupation and to remind people that Japan's crimes were not committed that long ago and should not be forgotten.

Similar to the Korean grandmothers, Filipino "Lolas" have their own Grandmother house with a collection of their testimonies. Also two of them have published two autobiographic books: *Comfort Woman: Slave of Destiny* by [Rosa Henson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosa_Henson) and *The Hidden Battle of Leyte: The Picture Diary of a Girl Taken by the Japanese Military* by Remedios Felias. This second book was written in the 1990s, after Lila Filipina was formed.

In [Bulacan](252408.md), there is an empty villa house [Bahay na Pula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahay_na_Pula) (meaning Red House in English) which was seized by Japanese soldiers during WWII and had been used as a comfort station where Filipino women were raped and held as comfort women.[^cite_note-298] The Bahay na Pula is seen as a memorial to the forgotten Filipino comfort women in the Philippines.

On December 8, 2017, the '[Filipina Comfort Women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipina_Comfort_Women)' statue by artist Jonas Roces was installed in [Baywalk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baywalk), [Roxas Boulevard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roxas_Boulevard) in Manila. About four months later, the statue was removed by government officials due to a "drainage improvement project" along the Baywalk.[^cite_note-299] It was later declared missing in 2019 when the statue artist Jonas Roces failed to deliver the statue for its reinstallation at the [Baclaran Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baclaran_Church).[^cite_note-300]

In 2019, a similar memorial statue in a Catholic-run shelter for the elderly and the homeless in San Pedro, Laguna was removed only two days after it was unveiled to the public. The bronze statue of a young woman with fists resting on her lap was removed without explanation and notice. The move came after the Japanese embassy complained.[^cite_note-301]

### United States

In 2010, the first American monument dedicated to the comfort women was established in [Palisades Park, New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palisades_Park,_New_Jersey).[^cite_note-NYT_Semple12-227]

On March 8, 2013, Bergen County erected a comfort women memorial on the lawn of the [Bergen County Courthouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bergen_County_Court_House) in [Hackensack, New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hackensack,_New_Jersey).[^cite_note-302]

In 2013, a memorial statue to comfort women called *[Peace Monument of Glendale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_Monument_of_Glendale)* was established in [Glendale, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glendale,_California).[^cite_note-latimes1-253] The statue has been subject to multiple legal attempts to remove it.[^cite_note-303] A 2014 lawsuit seeking the statue's removal was dismissed.[^cite_note-304][^cite_note-305][^cite_note-306] In 2013, Mio Sugita, Yuzuru Nishida and Hiromu Nakamaru, three members of the Japanese House of Representatives, urged Glendale to take down the statue.[^cite_note-307]

On May 30, 2014, a memorial was dedicated behind the [Fairfax County Government Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairfax_County_Government_Center) in Virginia.[^cite_note-308]

On August 16, 2014, a new memorial statue honoring the comfort women was unveiled in [Southfield, Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southfield,_Michigan).[^cite_note-309][^cite_note-310][^cite_note-311]

In June 2017, [Brookhaven, Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brookhaven,_Georgia) unveiled a statue memorializing the Comfort Women of World War II.[^cite_note-312]

On September 22, 2017, in an initiative led by the local Chinese-American community, [San Francisco](49728.md) erected a privately funded [San Francisco Comfort Women Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Francisco_Comfort_Women_Memorial) to the comfort women of World War II.[^cite_note-313][^cite_note-nytimes_sf_osaka_cut_ties-314] Some Japanese and Japanese-American opponents of the initiative argue the statue would promote hatred and anti-Japanese sentiment throughout the community and object to the statue singling out Japan.[^cite_note-315] [Tōru Hashimoto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C5%8Dru_Hashimoto), the mayor of [Osaka](52230.md), [Japan](15573.md), objected that the memorial should be "broadened to memorialize all the women who have been sexually assaulted and abused by soldiers of countries in the world".[^cite_note-316] Supporting the statue, Heather Knight of the [San Francisco Chronicle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Francisco_Chronicle) pointed to the [San Francisco Holocaust Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_Memorial_at_California_Palace_of_the_Legion_of_Honor) and the landmarked [Japanese internment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internment_of_Japanese_Americans) camps in California as evidence that Japan is "not being singled out".[^cite_note-317] In protest over the statue, Osaka ended the [sister city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sister_city) relationship with San Francisco that had been established since 1957.[^cite_note-nytimes_sf_osaka_cut_ties-314] When the city accepted the statue as public property in 2018, the mayor of Osaka sent a 10-page letter to the mayor of San Francisco, complaining of inaccuracies and unfairly singling out Japan for criticism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCurry2018-228]

A 2010 proposal to create a memorial in [Koreatown, Fort Lee, New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koreatown,_Fort_Lee) has been controversial and was undecided as of 2017.[^cite_note-318] On May 23, 2018, a comfort women memorial was installed in Constitution Park in Fort Lee.[^cite_note-319] Youth Council of Fort Lee, a student organization led by Korean American high school students in Fort Lee designed the memorial.

### Germany

In March 2017, the first comfort women statue in Europe was erected in [Wiesent, Bavaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wiesent,_Bavaria), Germany. The statue was a replica of the bronze statue installed in front of the Japanese Embassy in Seoul. Another German city, [Freiburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freiburg), had planned to set up a comfort woman statue there but it was scrapped due to "strong obstruction and pressure" by Japan.[^cite_note-320]

### Canada

In 2016, the first statue in Canada devoted to comfort women was placed in [Toronto](64646.md).[^cite_note-321]

### Australia



Statue of Peace at Ashfield Uniting Church

A comfort women statue was unveiled in [Sydney](27862.md) in August 2016. The 1.5-metre statue imported from Korea was originally meant for a public park in [Strathfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strathfield), but local council rejected it. [Reverend Bill Crews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Crews_\(minister\)) then agreed to install the statue outside his church, [Ashfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashfield,_New_South_Wales) [Uniting Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uniting_Church_in_Australia). He said, "It's finally found a home."[^cite_note-322]

### New Zealand

In April 2026, an [Auckland Council](22312574.md) local board voted against a proposal to install a comfort women memorial statue following public consultation and objections raised by Japanese officials, with the consultation process indicating a "lack of community support for the proposal".[^cite_note-323]

## Notable former comfort women

Former comfort women have provided public accounts of their experiences:

- Dutch East Indies – [Jan Ruff O'Herne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_Ruff_O'Herne) (1923–2019);

  [^cite_note-325]

  &#x20;[Lee Yong-soo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Yong-soo_\(activist\)) (1928–);

  [^cite_note-vday-326]

  [^cite_note-327]

  &#x20;[Song Sin-do](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Song_Sin-do) (1922–2017);

  [^cite_note-328]

  &#x20;Yoo Hee-nam (1927–2016);

  [^cite_note-329]

  [^cite_note-330]

  &#x20;[Kim Bok-dong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Bok-dong) (1926–2019)

  [^cite_note-331]

## Culture

### Art

Main article: [Comfort women in the arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comfort_women_in_the_arts)

- *[Red Angel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Angel_\(Movie\))* is a 1966 Japanese war drama film by [Yasuzō Masumura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasuzo_Masumura) where there are scenes of comfort women.

  [^cite_note-Red_Angel_–_Yasuzō_Masumura-335]

- *[City of Life and Death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Life_and_Death)* is a 2009 Chinese film written and directed by Lu Chuan. The movie is based on the [Nanjing Massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanjing_Massacre) that took place during the [Second Sino-Japanese War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Sino-Japanese_War). Several scenes of Chinese women tearfully volunteering themselves as comfort women to save the rest of the refugees are depicted, as well as their plight, pain and eventual death.

- *[Snowy Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snowy_Road)* is a 2015 South Korean film that tells the story about two teenage girls who are taken away from their homes and forced to become comfort women for the Japanese.

- *[Spirits' Homecoming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spirits'_Homecoming)* is a 2016 South Korean period drama film about comfort women.

  [^cite_note-336]

- *[I Can Speak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I_Can_Speak)* is a 2017 South Korean comedy-drama film starring [Na Moon-hee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Na_Moon-hee) as an elderly woman who travels to the United States to testify about her experience as a comfort woman.

  [^cite_note-337]

- *[Herstory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herstory_\(film\))* is a 2018 South Korean drama film based on a real-life story of three comfort women and seven other victims during the *Gwanbu Trial* which took place in [Shimonoseki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shimonoseki) in 1992.

  [^cite_note-338]

- *[How We Disappeared](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/How_We_Disappeared)* is a 2019 novel by [Jing-Jing Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jing-Jing_Lee) about a Singaporean woman forced into sexual slavery by the Japanese occupiers.

  [^cite_note-339]

  [^cite_note-340]

- *[Pulang Araw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulang_Araw)*, is a 2024 Filipino war drama series where there are scenes of comfort women.

  [^cite_note-341]

### Media

- *[A Secret Buried for 50 Years](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Secret_Buried_for_50_Years?action=edit\&redlink=1)* is a 1998 documentary about the stories of 13 comfort women in Taiwan.

  [^cite_note-342]

- *[Within Every Woman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Within_Every_Woman)* is a 2012 documentary by Canadian filmmaker [Tiffany Hsiung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiffany_Hsiung) on the Japanese comfort women program.

- *Thirty-Two* is a 2014 Chinese documentary about the lives of the 32 still-living Chinese comfort women.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPan2025157-273]

- *[The Apology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Apology_\(2016_film\))* is a 2016 documentary about three former comfort women seeking justice and stating their story.

  [^cite_note-Gracia-343]

- *Twenty-Two* is a 2017 Chinese documentary about the lives of the 22 still-living Chinese comfort women.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPan2025153-55]

- *[Tomorrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tomorrow_\(South_Korean_TV_series\))* Episode 13 of the Korean Netflix series Tomorrow explores the traumatic experiences of the comfort women.

  [^cite_note-344]

  &#x20;The fate of three of these women is the focus of the 60-minute episode Spring, with the peace monument in front of the Japanese embassy in Seoul also playing a significant role.

- *[Kokosuni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kokosuni)*, is a 2022 [KBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Broadcasting_System) documentary film by reporter Lee Seok-jae, on the revisionist histories of comfort women.

  [^cite_note-KGS-345]

[^cite_note-56]: &#x20;*Burning of Confidential Documents by Japanese Government*, case no.43, serial 2, International Prosecution Section vol. 8.[Clancey 1948](#CITEREFClancey1948), p. 1135: "When it became apparent that Japan would be forced to surrender, an organized effort was made to burn or otherwise destroy all documents and other evidence of ill-treatment of prisoners of war and civilian internees. The Japanese Minister of War issued an order on 14 August 1945 to all Army headquarters that confidential documents should be destroyed by fire immediately. On the same day, the Commandant of the Kempetai sent out instructions to the various Kempetai Headquarters detailing the methods of burning large quantities of documents efficiently."[Yoshimi 2000](#CITEREFYoshimi2000), p. 91: "\[...], the actual number of comfort women remains unclear because the Japanese army incinerated many crucial documents right after the defeat for fear of war crimes prosecution, \[...]".[Bix 2000](#CITEREFBix2000), p. 528[Drea 2006](#CITEREFDrea2006), p. 9: "Between the announcement of a ceasefire on August 15, 1945, and the arrival of small advance parties of American troops in Japan on August 28, Japanese military and civil authorities systematically destroyed military, naval, and government archives, much of which was from the period 1942–1945. Imperial General Headquarters in Tokyo dispatched enciphered messages to field commands throughout the Pacific and East Asia ordering units to burn incriminating evidence of war crimes, especially offenses against prisoners of war. The director of Japan's Military History Archives of the National Institute for Defense Studies estimated in 2003 that as much as 70 percent of the army's wartime records were burned or otherwise destroyed."

[^cite_note-Banning_Ploeg-67]: &#x20;Jan, Banning. ["'Comfort Woman' Ellen van der Ploeg passed away"](http://www.janbanning.com/comfort-woman-ellen-van-der-ploeg-passed-away/). *Jan Banning*. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20160105033245/http://www.janbanning.com/comfort-woman-ellen-van-der-ploeg-passed-away/) from the original on January 5, 2016. Ellen van der Ploeg, 84, from the Netherlands. During World War II, she lived with her family in the former Dutch East Indies (now Indonesia). Between 1943 and 1946, date at which she was liberated, Ellen lived in five different internment camps. When she was working in one of the camps, she was turned over to a comfort station by the Imperial Japanese forces. Soldiers would cut her food rationing if she did not work hard enough. They also ignored orders to use condoms, which led to her contracting a venereal disease.
